3.1
Chiral spin waves

In the following, we first focus on the excitations of the collinear ferromagnetic order,
i.e.
, the spin waves. The Hamiltonian of a ferromagnet is governed by the exchange [Eq. (
1
)], dipolar [Eq. (
2
)], and DM [Eq. (
3
)] interaction, as summarized in Eq. (
4
). Magnetic order breaks time-reversal symmetry and the spins in an excited state precess around the (effective) magnetic field in an anticlockwise fashion. In finite size magnets such as films and spheres, they display spatial chirality determined by magnetization, propagation, and surface normal directions. This is the essence of the Damon-Eshbach (DE) spin waves
[
14
,
13
]
in the magnetostatic approximation (Sec.
3.1.1
). The vector product in the DMI Eq. (
3
) obviously causes spatial chirality (Sec.
3.1.2
). Dipolar interaction and DMI are therefore two key tunable factors to realize non-reciprocal functionality in magnetic devices and metamaterials such as multilayers.

3.1.1
Damon-Eshbach spin waves

The excitations of the magnetic order at the surfaces of slabs or extremals of particles with wave numbers normal to the magnetic order are the archetypal chiral waves that were predicted more than 70 years ago. Here we sketch the essentials of the derivation and the properties of these modes and introduce a Hamiltonian formulation that will be useful in later Chapters.

Basis
.—Magnetostatic waves of thick in-plane magnetized films are governed by the dipolar interaction that dominates over exchange interaction in the long-wavelength limit
[
13
,
14
,
207
]
. Among them, the DE spin waves are those propagating nearly perpendicular to the magnetization and exponentially localized at the equator of spheres
[
13
]
or the surfaces of slabs
[
14
]
and are chiral
[
13
,
14
]
. The chirality can be traced to the interaction of the waves with their own stray magnetic fields caused by surface and bulk magnetic charges that must self-consistently be taken into account. Spin waves with in-plane wave vectors nearly perpendicular to the magnetization can then propagate only in one direction that is fixed by the outer product of surface normal
\hat{\bf n}
and magnetization direction
\hat{\bf M}
,
i.e.
, characterized by the chirality index
Z=\hat{\bf n}\cdot(\hat{\bf M}\times\hat{\bf k})
.

Figure 14:
DE surface modes with a unidirectional propagation direction perpendicular to the magnetization in the in-plane magnetized films [(a)] and magnetic spheres [(b)].

We focus on the configuration in Fig.
14
(a), where the film surface is perpendicular to the
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
-direction, the equilibrium magnetization
{\bf M}_{s}=M_{s}\hat{\mathbf{z}}
points along the
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
-direction parallel to an applied magnetic field
H_{z}\hat{\bf z}
, and we address the spin waves that propagate along the
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
-direction. The film thickness is
d
. We disregard here the crystalline anisotropy and exchange interaction, which is an excellent approximation for the long-wavelength spin waves in magnets such as yttrium iron garnet (YIG). Then, the magnetic free energy functional of magnetization
{\bf M}

F=-{\mu_{0}}\int d\mathbf{r}\left[M_{z}H_{z}+\frac{\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{r})}{8\pi}\cdot\nabla\int d\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})}{|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|}\right]

(13)

governs the effective magnetic field

\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{r})=-(1/\mu_{0}){\delta F}\left[\mathbf{M}\right]/{\delta\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{r})
in the Landau-Lifshitz equation
[
208
]

{\partial\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{r})}/{\partial t}=-\gamma\mu_{0}\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{r})\times\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{r}).

(14)

Here,
\gamma
denotes the modulus of the gyromagnetic ratio, and
\mu_{0}
is the vacuum permeability.
Small amplitude fluctuations are transverse to the equilibrium magnetization (
\mathbf{M}_{s}=M_{s}\hat{\mathbf{z}}
). They propagate with in-plane momenta
\mathbf{k}=k_{y}\hat{\mathbf{y}}+k_{z}\hat{\mathbf{z}}
and amplitudes

\mathbf{M}_{\gamma=x,y}^{j\mathbf{k}}=m_{\gamma}^{j\mathbf{k}}(x)e^{ik_{y}y}e^{ik_{z}z}.

(15)

Into the film:

\displaystyle m_{x}^{j\mathbf{k}}(x)=a_{j\mathbf{k}}e^{i\kappa_{j}x}+b_{j\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\kappa_{j}x},\hskip 28.45274ptm_{y}^{j\mathbf{k}}(x)=c_{j\mathbf{k}}e^{i\kappa_{j}x}+d_{j\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\kappa_{j}x},

(16)

where
j
is an integer when
d
is finite. The coefficients and wave numbers depend on the boundary conditions from the Maxwell equations (the tangential
{\bf H}_{S}
at the surface is continuous, and the normal
{\bf B}_{S}
at the surface is continuous).
To leading order in small
m
, the Landau-Lifshitz equation leads to the dispersion relation
[
14
]

\omega_{j\mathbf{k}}=\sqrt{\omega_{H}^{2}+\omega_{H}\omega_{M}\frac{\kappa_{j}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}{\kappa_{j}^{2}+|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}},

(17)

where
\kappa_{j}
solves the characteristic equation

(\beta k_{y})^{2}+\kappa_{j}^{2}(\alpha+1)^{2}-|\mathbf{k}|^{2}-2\kappa_{j}|\mathbf{k}|(\alpha+1)\cot(\kappa_{j}d)=0.

(18)

Here,
\omega_{H}=\gamma\mu_{0}H_{z}
,
\omega_{M}=\gamma\mu_{0}M_{s}
,
\alpha=\omega_{H}\omega_{M}/(\omega_{H}^{2}-\omega_{j\mathbf{k}}^{2})
, and
\beta=\omega_{j\mathbf{k}}\omega_{M}/(\omega_{H}^{2}-\omega_{j\mathbf{k}}^{2})
.

We refer to the solutions with purely imaginary
\kappa_{j}=iq_{x}
as DE modes
[
14
]
. For
{\bf k}=k_{y}\hat{\bf y}
they are highly degenerate because their frequency
\omega_{\mathbf{k}}
approaches a constant
\sqrt{\omega_{H}^{2}+\omega_{H}\omega_{M}}
in the limit of large momenta
|k_{y}|\gtrsim 1/d
. In the film,

\displaystyle m_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)

\displaystyle=C\left[e^{-q_{x}x}(-\alpha q_{x}+\beta k_{y})+De^{q_{x}(x+d)}(\alpha q_{x}+\beta k_{y})\right],

(19a)

\displaystyle m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)

\displaystyle=iC\left[e^{-q_{x}x}(-\beta q_{x}+\alpha k_{y})+De^{q_{x}(x+d)}(\beta q_{x}+\alpha k_{y})\right],

(19b)

in which
C
is a normalization constant, and
D=[{q_{x}(\alpha+1)-\beta k_{y}+\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}]/[{q_{x}(\alpha+1)+\beta k_{y}-\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}]
.

q_{x}
solves the characteristic equation
[
14
]
,

(\beta k_{y})^{2}-q_{x}^{2}(\alpha+1)^{2}-\left|\mathbf{k}\right|^{2}-2q_{x}|\mathbf{k}|(\alpha+1)\coth(q_{x}d)=0.

(20)

We observe that the DE modes are exponentially localized at the upper and lower surfaces depending on their propagation direction. Note that
x<0
in the coordinate system of Fig.
14
(a) and
\beta<0
for the DE modes. When
e^{q_{x}d}\gg 1
,
\coth(q_{x}d)\rightarrow 1
, and Eq. (
20
) becomes

q_{x}(\alpha+1)+\left|\mathbf{k}\right|\approx|\beta k_{y}|.

(21)

When
k_{y}<0
,
D\rightarrow 0
and
m_{x,y}^{\bf k}\propto e^{-q_{x}x}
is localized at the lower surface, for
k_{y}>0
at the upper surface. Moreover, the DE mode is (almost) circularly polarized. For example, for surface magnons with
k_{y}<0
and
e^{q_{x}d}\gg 1
,
im_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}+({k_{y}}/{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|})m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}\approx 0
,

i.e.
, when
\mathbf{k}=k_{y}\hat{\mathbf{y}}
the DE magnons are circularly polarized
m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}\approx im_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}
. The DE-like modes with
k_{z}\neq 0
acquire a finite ellipticity. Equation (
20
) implies that these modes preserve their surface character and chirality as long as
\left|k_{z}\right|<\left|k_{y}\right|\sqrt{M_{s}/H_{z}}

[
14
]
.

The eigenmodes (
19a
) and (
19b
) are suitable basis functions into which to expand the free energy Eq. (
13
) and formulate a Hamiltonian function. The latter can be easily quantized
[
211
,
210
,
209
,
212
]
by replacing the magnetization amplitudes by spin operators
\mathbf{M}\rightarrow-\gamma\hbar\hat{\mathbf{S}}
(and
M_{s}=\gamma\hbar S
). In extended systems where the latter can be expressed in terms of magnons with wave vector
\mathbf{k}
and operators
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathbf{k}},\hat{\alpha}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}
by the leading order term of the Holstein-Primakoff expansion
[
211
,
210
,
209
,
212
]
:

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{x,y}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2S}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\left(m_{x,y}^{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{r})\hat{\alpha}_{\mathbf{k}}+m_{x,y}^{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{r})^{\ast}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}\right),

(22a)

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{z}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=-S+(\hat{S}_{x}^{2}+\hat{S}_{y}^{2})/(2S).

(22b)

With the following normalization of the eigenmodes
[
13
,
213
]
,

\int d\mathbf{r}\left(m_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{r})m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{r})^{\ast}-m_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{r})^{\ast}m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{r})\right)=-i/2,

(23)

we arrive at a magnon Hamiltonian as a sum of harmonic oscillators
\hat{H}_{m}=\sum_{\bf k}\hbar\omega_{\bf k}\hat{\alpha}_{\bf k}^{\dagger}\hat{\alpha}_{\bf k}
.

In finite magnets, the wave vector is not conserved and the eigenmodes can be expressed on a symmetry-adapted basis. Walker
[
13
]
solved the magnetostatic problem for magnetic spheres before Damon and Eshbach
[
14
]
addressed the slab geometry. The surface modes in the sphere with magnetization pointing to the north pole propagate along the equator with angular momenta
l\gg 1
in only one direction, as illustrated in Fig.
14
(b). They are strongly localized and circularly polarized with amplitudes
[
13
]

\displaystyle m_{\phi}=im_{\rho}=\left(\frac{l}{\pi}\right)^{3/4}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar\gamma M_{s}}{R^{3}}}\left(\frac{\rho}{R}\right)^{l-1}.

(24)

Their wavelength
\lambda\sim R/l
and frequency

\displaystyle\omega_{\rm DE}=\gamma\mu_{0}\left(H_{z}-\frac{M_{s}}{3}+\frac{lM_{s}}{2l+1}\right)

(25)

saturates at
\omega_{\rm DE}\rightarrow\gamma\mu_{0}\left(H_{z}+{M_{s}}/{6}\right)
when
l\gg 1
, very similar to the DE modes for larger
k
.

In spite of the long history, the topology of Damon-Eshbach modes was addressed only recently by Yamamoto
et al.

[
33
]
. In contrast to the conventional bulk-edge correspondence, their frequency band does not overlap with the bulk modes and therefore does not require gap formation, as shown in Fig.
15
(a) for the dispersion of spin waves in YIG slabs. The topology of Damon-Eshbach modes of the infinite half-space is characterized by vortex lines and corresponding winding numbers in reciprocal space. This theory also provides topological characterizations of Hamiltonians for other classical waves.

The Damon-Eshbach surface modes were also theoretically predicted in antiferromagnet long ago
[
214
,
215
,
216
,
217
,
218
,
219
]
, but still without experimental confirmation. Recently, Liu
et al
. showed that dipolar spin waves in uniaxial easy-axis antiferromagnets are naturally in a topological nodal-line semimetal phase
[
220
]
. The topological surface modes reside in the dipolar-induced gap between the bulk bands, different from its ferromagnet counterpart
[
33
]
, and display a chirality-momentum locking
[
220
]
. For bulk antiferromagnets, Shen predicted that the dipole-dipole interaction acts as an effective spin-orbit coupling between magnons with opposite polarization (effective spin) that induces such as the
D’yakonov-Perel’-type magnon spin relaxation
and the intrinsic magnon (inverse) spin Hall effect
[
221
]
.

Functionalities by chirality
.—Unidirectional propagation of waves provides unique functionalities that can be useful in magnetic, optic, mechanical, and electronic devices. As indicated in Fig.
15
(b), the surface modes of sufficiently thick magnetic films transport heat in a particular direction, even against a temperature gradient,
i.e.
, they act like a “heat conveyer-belt”
[
222
,
223
,
224
,
225
]
. Figure
15
(c) shows that the magnetization direction controls the temperature gradients of the heat generated by microwave absorption
[
222
]
.

YIG magnetic spheres are also excellent optical resonators that support whispering gallery photon modes as sketched in Fig.
15
(d). Sharma
et al
. predicted that the surface magnons can strongly interact with optical whispering gallery modes
[
226
,
227
,
228
]
due to the surface nature
[
212
,
229
]
.
By angular momentum conservation in the inelastic scattering of a whispering-gallery photon by magnons, surface magnons can contribute only by reflection when tuned to the anti-Stokes line with full suppression of the Stokes scattering, which improves the efficiency of optical cooling of magnons
[
230
]
. The efficiency also depends on the spatial overlap between photon and magnon modes and can be enhanced by tuning the systems to magnon modes that are still chiral but slightly separated from the surface, which is possible in a regime in which the exchange interaction contributes
[
229
]
. These modes are also less prone to dephasing by surface roughness scattering
[
231
]
discussed below.

Figure 15:
Damon-Eshbach configurations. (a) illustrates the spin waves propagating perpendicular to the in-plane magnetization of thick YIG films with the film thickness
\sim 100
nm. For small wave numbers
k
the modes are governed by the dipolar interaction and the Damon-Eshbach treatment holds
[
14
]
. At large
k
the exchange interaction dominates and dispersion is parabolic. The dipolar-exchange waves are those with intermediate
k
. The frequencies range from gigahertz to many terahertz. (b)-(c) describes the heat conveyer effect by the Damon-Eshbach modes that can unidirectionally transmit angular momentum even against the temperature gradient. (d) is the spherical magnetic resonator in which the optical whispering gallery
modes are excited by proximity optical fiber. The figures (b)-(d) are taken from Refs.
[
222
,
212
]
.

Surface waves should be sensitive to surface roughness. The topological protection that implies a complete absence of backscattering for sufficiently thick films
[
33
,
232
]
does not mean that surface disorder has no effect
[
231
]
because
forward scattering is still allowed. Typically, the Gilbert damping is enhanced hundreds of times for the Damon Eshbach modes in YIG films of micrometers in thickness and surface roughness of several nanometers
[
231
]
. Thus, for the long-range disorder, the transport is much less affected by the suppressed backscattering (vertex correction). The wavelength of the Damon-Eshbach modes
\sim 2\pi d
should be much larger than the exchange wavelength
\lambda_{\rm ex}
, leading to
d\gg\lambda_{\rm ex}/(2\pi)
. Thus, the surface modes do not exist in sufficiently thin films.
Their group velocity tends to be zero when the linear momentum is larger than the inverse of film thickness, leading
to a small spin conductivity. These may limit the applications of Damon–Eshbach spin waves in magnonic devices.

The polarization of the DE spin waves at one surface and the dipolar field of the DE modes of the other surface does not match (see also in Sec.
3.2.2
below), so the DE modes at the two opposite surfaces do not really interact via direct dipolar interaction due to the surface disorder
[
231
]
.
In films with finite thickness, the disorder can induce back scattering between the surface modes at both surfaces, which break topological protection and reduce the conductivity of the surface modes. Nevertheless, Mohseni
et al.

[
232
]
reported strongly suppressed backscattering of dipole-exchange magnetostatic surface spin waves even in thin films.

3.1.2
Chiral spin waves by spin-orbit interaction

Besides the dipolar origin addressed above, another source of chirality of spin waves originates from the relativistic spin-orbit interaction.
The broken inversion symmetry at the interfaces of ferromagnets gives rise to the interface Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DMI) interaction Eq. (
3
). The coefficient
D
is a spin-orbit interaction that can be enhanced at the interface to a heavy metal such as Pt that mediates the hopping between neighboring spins
{\bf S}_{i}
and
{\bf S}_{j}
. Interfacial DMI causes non-reciprocal spin-wave propagation, such as different group velocities for spin waves of the same frequency that propagate in opposite directions
[
233
]
.

Now consider a magnetic film with the surface normal along the
\hat{\bf x}
-direction and the saturated magnetization along the
\hat{\bf z}
-direction.
The associated interfacial DMI
in the continuum model
reads

[
233
]

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{DMI}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{D}{2\mu_{0}M_{s}^{2}}\int d\mathbf{r}{\bf M}\cdot[(\hat{\bf x}\times\nabla)\times{\bf M}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{D}{2\mu_{0}M_{s}^{2}}\int d\mathbf{r}\left[\hat{\mathbf{y}}\cdot(\mathbf{M}\times\partial_{z}\mathbf{M})-\hat{\mathbf{z}}\cdot(\mathbf{M}\times\partial_{y}\mathbf{M})\right],

(26)

where
D
is the DMI constant. For the spin waves propagating perpendicular to the magnetization, the magnon dispersion limited by the exchange and DM interactions to leading order reads

\displaystyle\omega_{k_{y}}=\mu_{0}\gamma H_{\mathrm{z}}+\omega_{M}\alpha_{\mathrm{ex}}k_{y}^{2}-\gamma Dk_{y}/M_{s},

(27)

where
\alpha_{\rm ex}
is the exchange stiffness. The DMI then causes a rigid shift of the parabolic band, as illustrated in Fig.
16
(a). For propagation along the general direction with momentum
{\bf k}
, the dispersion is governed by the surface normal
\hat{\bf n}
, the magnetization direction
\hat{\bf M}_{s}
and the momentum that constructs a chirality by the right-hand rule,

\displaystyle\omega_{{\bf k}}=\mu_{0}\gamma H_{\mathrm{z}}+\omega_{M}\alpha_{\mathrm{ex}}{\bf k}^{2}-\gamma D/M_{s}(\hat{\bf n}\times\hat{\bf M}_{s})\cdot{\bf k},

(28)

i.e.
, there is no non-reciprocity for spin waves propagating along the magnetization direction. The chirality index
(\hat{\bf n}\times\hat{\bf M}_{s})\cdot{\bf k}
naturally appears in the dispersion relation.
Additional numerical calculations showed that the non-reciprocity affects not only
the frequency but also the amplitude and the attenuation length
[
234
,
235
]
.

The impact of the interfacial DMI
|D|
on all but the surface spin waves decreases with the magnetic film thickness
[
237
,
236
]
. For thin ferromagnetic films in contact with heavy metals typically
D\sim 1~{\rm mJ/m^{2}}
(techniques for measuring
D
and its values for different materials have been reviewed in
[
238
]
). The DMI also favors out-of-plane magnetization since the DMI vector
{\bf D}
is perpendicular to the film normal and when strong enough may pull the magnetization out of the film plane (refer to Sec.
2.1.2
). The system which exhibits large interfacial DMI usually contains heavy metal adjacent to the ferromagnet layer. The heavy metal may be notorious for spin wave application as it will introduce additional damping in magnetization dynamics and curb the long-distance propagation of the spin waves. Recently, it has been reported that DMI can also arise in the absence of heavy metal. All-oxide interface can provide a sizable DMI, which extends the material set of chiral magnetism
[
239
,
240
]
.

Recently, a significant DMI was reported for the interface between GGG and rare earth iron garnets
[
241
]
. Chiral velocities were observed in thin YIG film of thickness
7
nm on GGG substrate in the measurement via the microwave transmission between two strip lines on YIG, which were interpreted by the above DMI-shift with
D=-16~\mu{\rm J}/{\rm m^{2}}

[
242
]
. A competitive mechanism such as the Doppler shift by the stripline chirality (introduced in Sec.
3.2.1
below)
[
59
]
in the nonlinear regime and chiral damping by the proximity metallic contact in the linear response (reviewed in Sec.
4.3.2
)
[
243
]
provides the same phenomenology. A similar mechanism was employed to interpret the experimentally observed non-reciprocity in electrical injection and detection of incoherent magnons of YIG films with thickness
50
nm in Fig.
16
(b), where
D=-2~\mu{\rm J}/{\rm m^{2}}
was fitted
[
244
]
. However, the DMI can be eliminated by a global gauge transformation in terms of a phase that has no physical consequences on transport in that the group velocity of the spin waves is the same at the same frequency. A similar argument applies to the absence of equilibrium spin current under the Rashba spin-orbit coupling. The possibility of the DMI between YIG and GGG awaits first-principle calculation for the origin of the DMI by closed-shell magnetic moments with small spin-orbit interaction.

Figure 16:
Non-reciprocity observed experimentally in electrical injection and detection of incoherent magnons of YIG films. (a) illustrates the shift of dispersion by the DMI. In (b), the left and right platinum electrodes detect the diffusive magnon injected by the middle platinum electrode via the spin Hall effect. The difference of the signals detected by the left and right platinum depends on the magnetization direction
\alpha
(relative to the electrode wire
\hat{\bf x}
-direction) by
\propto\sin^{2}\alpha\cos\alpha
, as shown in (c). The figures are taken from Ref.
[
244
]
.

3.2
Chiral magnetic fields

A plane wave with (real) frequency
\omega
and a three-dimensional complex wave vector
k
is called propagating when
k
is real and evanescent when imaginary. Surface or interface wave propagates in a plane but is evanescent normal to it, therefore natural conduits of guided transport in a plane. In wave guides only the wave vector component along its axes is real and propagation is in one dimension only. Here we focus on the electrodynamics of magnetic fields with evanescent components that are chiral.

3.2.1
Stripline

A stripline is a metallic wire biased by an ac electric current. It generates Ampére magnetic fields,
e.g.
, Ref.
[
206
]
with interesting chiralities that have been overlooked in the past.

Let us focus on the short stripline in Fig.
17
(a) of length
l
along
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
and rectangular cross section
w\delta
, with width
w
and thickness
\delta
, supported by a planar substrate. An ac current density bias
\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{r},\omega)\parallel\hat{\bf z}
with frequency
\omega
generates the vector potential
[
206
]

\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{r},\omega)=\frac{\mu_{0}}{4\pi}\int d\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\omega)\frac{e^{ik|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|}}{|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|},

(29)

where
\mu_{0}
is the vacuum permeability and
k=\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2}}=\omega/c
. The magnetic field
(H_{x},H_{y})=(1/\mu_{0})(\partial A_{z}/\partial y,\\
-\partial A_{z}/\partial x)
decays as a function of distance
r
from the stripline by
\sim 1/r
and
H_{z}=0
. To see that this magnetic field is evanescent and chiral we substitute the Weyl identity
[
17
]
,

\frac{e^{ik\sqrt{\left(x-x^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(y-y^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(z-z^{\prime}\right)^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\left(x-x^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(y-y^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(z-z^{\prime}\right)^{2}}}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int dk_{y}dk_{z}\frac{e^{ik_{x}|x-x^{\prime}|+ik_{y}(y-y^{\prime})+ik_{z}(z-z^{\prime})}}{k_{x}},

(30)

where
k_{x}=\sqrt{(\omega/c)^{2}-k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2}}
.

Figure 17:
A stripline of rectangular section with length
l
, width
w
and thickness
\delta
supported by a planar substrate [(a)] and biased by an ac current. (b) illustrates the polarization of the Ampére magnetic field in the momentum space.

In the mixed real and Fourier space

\displaystyle H_{x}(x;k_{y},k_{z})

\displaystyle=2iJ(\omega)\frac{e^{-ik_{x}x}}{k_{x}}\frac{e^{ik_{x}\delta}-1}{k_{x}}\sin\left(k_{y}\frac{w}{2}\right)\frac{\sin(k_{z}l/2)}{k_{z}}e^{-ik_{z}z},

(31a)

\displaystyle H_{y}(x;k_{y},k_{z})

\displaystyle=2iJ(\omega)\frac{e^{-ik_{x}x}}{k_{x}}\frac{e^{ik_{x}\delta}-1}{k_{y}}\sin\left(k_{y}\frac{w}{2}\right)\frac{\sin(k_{z}l/2)}{k_{z}}e^{-ik_{z}z}.

(31b)

Hence

\displaystyle k_{x}H_{x}(x;k_{y},k_{z})=k_{y}H_{y}(x;k_{y},k_{z})

(32)

obeys a polarization (spin)-momentum locking relation. The propagation direction,
i.e.
, the rotation direction of the fields, is along the
\hat{\bf y}
-direction, but the polarization is along
\hat{\bf z}
, so the polarization (spin) of
H_{x,y}
is transverse. In Sec.
3.5
, we will strictly define the transverse spin density of the electromagnetic waves, where we find this implies the spin-momentum locking and chirality of the evanescent magnetic field.
For a long wire
l\gg w
, the magnetic field oscillates as function of
k_{z}
with
a short period of
4\pi/l
, while the dependence on
k_{y}
is slow,
i.e.
, with a periodicity of

4\pi/w
.

The evanescent limit
\sqrt{k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2}}\gg\omega/c
is typical for magnonics: Microwaves with frequencies of
\omega/(2\pi)\sim 1
GHz,
k_{0}\equiv\omega/c=2.1
m
-1
, and a characteristic wavelength
\lambda_{0}=2\pi/k_{0}=0.3
m. As long as we focus on wave lengths of interest that are much shorter than this scale,
k_{x}\rightarrow i\sqrt{k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2}}=i\kappa
. When additionally
\kappa\delta\ll 1
, the magnetic near-fields read

\displaystyle H_{x}(x;k_{y},k_{z})

\displaystyle=-2iJ(\omega)e^{\kappa x}\frac{e^{-\kappa\delta}-1}{\kappa^{2}}\sin\left(k_{y}\frac{w}{2}\right)\frac{\sin(k_{z}l/2)}{k_{z}}e^{-ik_{z}z},

(33a)

\displaystyle H_{y}(x;k_{y},k_{z})

\displaystyle=2J(\omega)e^{\kappa x}\frac{e^{-\kappa\delta}-1}{\kappa k_{y}}\sin\left(k_{y}\frac{w}{2}\right)\frac{\sin(k_{z}l/2)}{k_{z}}e^{-ik_{z}z},

(33b)

and thereby

H_{x}(x;k_{y},k_{z})=-i(k_{y}/\kappa)H_{y}(x;k_{y},k_{z}).

(34)

When
\left|k_{y}\right|\ll|k_{z}|
the radiation is nearly
linearly-polarized along the
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
-direction
(\left|H_{x}\right|\ll\left|H_{y}\right|)
.
On the other hand, when
\left|k_{y}\right|\gg|k_{z}|
,

H_{x}(x;k_{y},k_{z})\rightarrow-i\mathrm{sgn}(k_{y})H_{y}(x;k_{y},k_{z})
. The field is then
nearly right- (left-) circularly polarized for positive (negative)
k_{y}
,
i.e.
, propagating in positive and (negative) directions.
Figure
17
(b) summarizes the field polarization (spin) as a function of wave vector direction for
a short stripline. For a long stripline,
l\rightarrow\infty
and
k_{z}\rightarrow 0
, on the other hand, the polarization and momentum are locked. The chirality of the stripline magnet field causes the chiral pumping of circularly-polarized spin waves
[
64
,
65
,
67
,
66
]
as reviewed in the next Chapter (Sec.
4.3.1
).

We can understand the chirality best for an infinitely long wire,
e.g.
, along the
\hat{\bf z}
-direction, as depicted in the snapshot Fig.
18
and a plane
AB
with the surface normal along the
\hat{\bf x}
-direction. The magnetic field is indicated by its direction (red arrows) while its amplitude decays slowly like
1/r
. The polarization of the radial waves periodically changes sign, but not direction. On the line from
A
to
B
(to the left) the instantaneous vector rotates in a clockwise fashion, but anti-clockwise on the line from
B
to
A
. In the time domain, the spherical waves emitted by the stripline move outward, so the wave on the AB (BA) line has a wave vector to the left (right). Therefore, in a particular plane (that does not cross the stripline), the magnetic field is polarization-momentum locked, with a propagation direction governed by the outer product of the polarization and surface normal of the chosen plane,
i.e.
, the ac magnetic field is chiral. As discussed above the magnetic field wave on the AB plane is not periodic and is composed of a linear combination of evanescent plane waves.

Figure 18:
The chirality of an ac magnetic field emitted by an infinitely long stripline on a plane defined by
AB
with surface normal
{\bf n}
. The polarization-momentum locking is illustrated by the thick black circles with red (green) arrows for the field direction (momentum
{\bf k}
) at the bottom of the figure.

The chirality of the ac magnetic field implies a unidirectional launch of spin waves in the in-plane magnetized magnetic field below the stripline
[
64
,
65
,
67
]
, as we will review in Sec.
4.3.1
.

3.2.2
Dipolar stray magnetic fields of spin waves

Another example of evanescent ac magnetic fields is the stray field generated by spin waves. The field of a magnetic dipole decays faster than that of the stripline (monopole),
i.e.
, as
1/r^{3}
. We discuss in the following the chirality of the fields emitted by coherent spin waves in a magnetic film and a nanowire.

In-plane magnetized film
.—Spin waves cause periodic perturbations of the equilibrium magnetization and associated stray magnetic fields. Here we focus on the excitations of homogeneously in-plane magnetized magnetic films. Waves at GHz are in the (quasi-)static regime, which allows a treatment close to conventional electrostatics. A non-uniform magnetization in which the divergence of the magnetic vector field generates a magnetic charge density
\rho_{M}
:

\nabla\cdot{\bf M}\equiv\rho_{M}.

(35)

It is convenient to express the stray field in terms of the magnetic potential

{\bf H}=-\nabla\phi_{M},

(36)

which leads to the Poisson equation:

\nabla^{2}\phi_{M}=\nabla\cdot{\bf M}.

(37)

The stray field is the negative gradient of the magnetic potential

\phi_{M}({\bf r})=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\rm volume}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot{\bf M}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}dV^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\rm surface}\frac{{\bf n}\cdot{\bf M}({\bf r}^{\prime})}{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}dS^{\prime},

(38)

where we separated the contributions from “volume” and “surface” charges.
Figure
19
illustrates the physical origin of the chirality of the dipolar field
[
50
,
48
]
for a magnetization that is uniform across a (sufficiently) thin film of thickness
\delta
. Spin waves that are right circularly polarized and propagate perpendicular to the in-plane magnetization, generate both surface and bulk magnetic charges. Their contributions generated by plane waves that move to the right add up to generate a finite stray field above the film, but cancel below the film and vice versa for left-moving spin waves. The stray fields are therefore unidirectional with a handedness governed by the outer vector product of the surface normal and equilibrium magnetization directions. This mechanism is responsible for the chiral pumping of spin waves by nearby nanomagnets, as discussed in the following Sec.
4.2.2
.

Figure 19:
Dipolar fields generated by spin waves propagating perpendicular to the in-plane magnetization of a thin film. (a) illustrates the particular nature of the stray fields of surface and bulk magnetic charges that vanish on one side of the film depending on the wave vector of the spin wave. (b) summarizes the polarization (spin), propagation, and spatial distribution of the stray fields. The figure [(b)] is taken from Ref.
[
50
]
.

In general, spin waves are elliptically polarized and may propagate in any direction in the plane. We fix the equilibrium magnetization along the
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
-direction. A spin wave consists of a norm-conserving transverse magnetization amplitude
M
. Expressed as a superposition of right (
m_{R}
) and left (
m_{L}
) circularly polarized components,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}M_{x}(\mathbf{r},t)\\
M_{y}(\mathbf{r},t)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}m_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)\cos\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
-m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{R}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\cos\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
-\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\end{array}\right)+m_{L}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\cos\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\end{array}\right),

where
m_{R}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)=[m_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)+m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)]/2
,

m_{L}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)=[m_{x}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)-m_{y}^{\mathbf{k}}(x)]/2
,
\boldsymbol{\rho}=(y,z)
, and
\textbf{k}=(k_{y},k_{z}
).
This magnetization generates a dynamical dipolar field
\left(\alpha,\beta\in\{x,y,z\}\right)

h_{\beta}(\mathbf{r},t)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\int d\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\frac{M_{\alpha}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},t)}{|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|}.

(47)

By substituting into Eq. (
47
) and using the
Coulomb integral

I=\int d\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\frac{e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\prime}}f\left(x^{\prime}\right)}{|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|}=\frac{2\pi}{|{\bf k}|}e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\int dx^{\prime}e^{-\left|x-x^{\prime}\right||{\bf k}|}f\left(x^{\prime}\right),

(48)

their dipolar field outside the film reads

\displaystyle\textbf{h}(\mathbf{r},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\left(\left|\mathbf{k}\right|+\mathrm{sgn}(x)k_{y}\right)\cos\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
k_{y}\left(\frac{k_{y}}{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}+\mathrm{sgn}(x)\right)\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
k_{z}\left(\frac{k_{y}}{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}+\mathrm{sgn}(x)\right)\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\end{array}\right)e^{-\left|\mathbf{k}\right|\left|x\right|}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}m_{R}^{\mathbf{k}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)e^{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|\mathrm{sgn}(x)x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}(\left|\mathbf{k}\right|-\mathrm{sgn}(x)k_{y})\cos\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
k_{y}\left(-\frac{k_{y}}{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}+\mathrm{sgn}(x)\right)\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
k_{z}\left(-\frac{k_{y}}{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|}+\mathrm{sgn}(x)\right)\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\end{array}\right)e^{-\left|\mathbf{k}\right|\left|x\right|}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}m_{L}^{\mathbf{k}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)e^{\left|\mathbf{k}\right|\mathrm{sgn}(x)x^{\prime}},

where
\mathrm{sgn}(x)
is the sign function. A spin wave propagating normal to the magnetization
\left(k_{z}\rightarrow 0\right)
generates a dipolar field above the film with
h_{z}=0
and

[
48
,
50
]

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}h_{x}\\
h_{y}\end{array}\right)_{x>\delta}(\mathbf{r},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|k_{y}\right|+k_{y}}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\cos(k_{y}y-\omega t)\\
\sin(k_{y}y-\omega t)\end{array}\right)e^{-|k_{y}|x}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}m_{R}^{k_{y}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)e^{|k_{y}|x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left|k_{y}\right|-k_{y}}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\cos(k_{y}y-\omega t)\\
-\sin(k_{y}y-\omega t)\end{array}\right)e^{-|k_{y}|x}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}m_{L}^{k_{y}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)e^{|k_{y}|x^{\prime}},

and below the film

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}h_{x}\\
h_{y}\end{array}\right)_{x<0}(\mathbf{r},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|k_{y}\right|-k_{y}}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\cos(k_{y}y-\omega t)\\
\sin(k_{y}y-\omega t)\end{array}\right)e^{|k_{y}|x}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}m_{R}^{k_{y}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)e^{-\left|k_{y}\right|x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left|k_{y}\right|+k_{y}}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\cos(k_{y}y-\omega t)\\
-\sin(k_{y}y-\omega t)\end{array}\right)e^{|k_{y}|x}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}m_{L}^{k_{y}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)e^{-\left|k_{y}\right|x^{\prime}}.

The polarization of the dipolar field of circularly polarized spin waves is opposite to that of magnetization. Right (left) propagating spin waves with
k_{y}>0
(
k_{y}<0
) only generate a dipolar field above (below) the film. Spin waves propagating
parallel
to the equilibrium magnetization
\left(k_{y}\rightarrow 0\right)
generate the fields

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}h_{x}\\
h_{y}\end{array}\right)(\mathbf{r},t)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}|{k}_{z}|\cos\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\\
\mathrm{sgn}\left(x\right)k_{z}\sin\left(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}-\omega t\right)\end{array}\right)e^{-\left|{k}_{z}\right|\left|x\right|}\int_{0}^{\delta}dx^{\prime}\left(m_{R}^{{k}_{z}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)+m_{L}^{{k}_{z}}\left(x^{\prime}\right)\right)e^{|{k}_{z}|\mathrm{sgn}\left(x\right)x^{\prime}}.

Above the film, the dipolar field of spin waves with positive (negative)
k_{z}
, is always left (right) circularly polarized,
viz.
,
polarization-momentum locked. Below the film, the polarization is reversed.

This dipolar field can be detected directly by local probes such as nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamonds that are sensitive to weak magnetic fields and can be read out optically. Quantum impurity relaxometry
[
245
,
246
]
as illustrated in Fig.
20
(a) measures the relaxation rate of an NV center spin by its coupling with a magnetic noise source
[
247
,
248
,
249
,
250
]
. The chirality of the stray dipolar fields plays a role because of the selection rules sketched in Fig.
20
(b), the transitions from state
|0\rangle\rightarrow|-1\rangle
and
|0\rangle\rightarrow|+1\rangle
are triggered by left and right circularly polarized stray fields with different and observable rates
\Gamma_{-}
and
\Gamma_{+}
because the amplitudes of the two differ
[
50
]
. The modeling based on this mechanism successfully explains the recent experimental observations as a function of applied field
[
251
]
in Fig.
20
(c).

We review other physical consequences of the chiral stray field in Sec.
4.2
.

Figure 20:
Sensing of the chirality of magnetic stray field by quantum impurity (diamond NV center) relaxometry. In (a), NV centers (QI) are placed above the Py (and YIG) magnetic thin films, whose stray-field noise triggers the transitions between states under the selection rules sketched in (b). The chirality caused the difference in the observed transition rates in panels (c). The figures are taken from Ref.
[
251
]
.

Magnetic wire
.—The dipolar fields of an excited magnetic wire combine features of the ac current-biased stripline with the stray field from magnetic films. Here we focus on the chirality of the dipolar stray field generated by the ferromagnetic resonance (FMR) of a magnetic nanowire. We consider a nanowire and its equilibrium magnetization along the
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
direction. The magnetic oscillations are the real part of

\tilde{M}_{x,y}(\mathbf{r},t)=\tilde{m}_{x,y}\Theta(x)\Theta(-x+d)\Theta(y+w/2)\Theta(-y+w/2)e^{-i\omega t},

(71)

where
d
and
w
are the thickness and width of the nanowire and
\omega
is the FMR frequency. The
corresponding dipolar magnetic field

\tilde{h}_{\beta}(\mathbf{r},t)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\int\frac{\tilde{M}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime},t)}{|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|}d\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\int dz^{\prime}\int_{0}^{d}dx^{\prime}\int_{-w/2}^{w/2}dy^{\prime}\frac{\tilde{m}_{\alpha}e^{-i\omega t}}{\sqrt{z^{\prime 2}+(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}}}.

(72)

By substituting the Coulomb integral
[
48
,
17
]
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{z^{\prime 2}+(x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dk_{x}dk_{y}\frac{e^{-|z^{\prime}|\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}}}{\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}}e^{ik_{x}(x-x^{\prime})+ik_{y}(y-y^{\prime})},

(73)

the magnetic field with Fourier component
k_{y}
below the nanowire (
x<0
)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{\beta}(k_{y},x,t)

\displaystyle=\int h_{\beta}(\mathbf{r},t)e^{-ik_{y}y}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dk_{x}(k_{x}\tilde{m}_{x}+k_{y}\tilde{m}_{y})k_{\beta}e^{ik_{x}x-i\omega t}\frac{1}{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}\frac{1}{ik_{x}}(1-e^{-ik_{x}d})\frac{2\sin(k_{y}w/2)}{k_{y}}.

(74)

Closing the contour of the
k_{x}
integral in the lower half complex plane

\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\tilde{h}_{x}\\
\tilde{h}_{y}\end{array}\right)(k_{y},x,t)=-\frac{i}{4\pi}e^{\left|k_{y}\right|x}(1-e^{-\left|k_{y}\right|d})\frac{2\sin(k_{y}w/2)}{k_{y}\left|k_{y}\right|}\left(\begin{array}[c]{cc}\left|k_{y}\right|&ik_{y}\\
ik_{y}&-\left|k_{y}\right|\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\tilde{m}_{x}\\
\tilde{m}_{y}\end{array}\right)e^{-i\omega t}.

(75)

The dynamics of a cylindrical nanowire is right circularly polarized
\left(\tilde{m}_{y}=i\tilde{m}_{x}\right)
, which implies that the Fourier components of
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}
with
k_{y}>0
vanish,
i.e.
, being unidirectional, while that with
k_{y}<0
is left circularly polarized
\left(\tilde{h}_{y}=-i\tilde{h}_{x}\right)
.

3.2.3
Waveguide and cavity microwaves

Here we review chiral electromagnetic waves that emerge due to partial confinement which leads to waves that are partly evanescent and propagating only along particular directions. We discuss two examples in more detail,
i.e.
, a waveguide and a torus cavity
[
252
,
253
]
. We focus on the microwave regime, but the formalism can be used as well for THz and optical frequencies.

Waveguide
.—A waveguide confines electromagnetic fields in two directions, but allows propagation along a third one. Examples are co-planar waveguides that in their simplest form are three parallel strip lines on a dielectric surface. Here we focus on the confinement caused by hollow tubes of highly conducting metals. The eigenmodes are the solutions of Maxwell’s equations with appropriate boundary conditions.

The free-space Maxwell equations for the electric
E
and magnetic
H
fields in the frequency space

[
206
]

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=i\mu_{0}\omega{\bf H},

(76a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle=-i\varepsilon_{0}\omega\mathbf{E},

(76b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle=0,

(76c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=0,

(76d)

lead to the wave equations
[
206
]

\left(\nabla^{2}-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\right)\{\mathbf{E},\mathbf{B}\}=0.

(77)

The conducting boundary conditions at thick metallic walls are

\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{E}|_{S}=0,~~~~~~\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{H}|_{S}=0.

(78)

For a waveguide with translational symmetry along
\hat{\bf z}
, we separate the transverse and longitudinal components of the fields to be
\{{\bf E}_{t},{\bf H}_{t}\}
and
\{{\bf E}_{z},{\bf H}_{z}\}
, respectively.
The modes are divided into two classes,
viz.
, transverse magnetic (TM) modes with
{\bf B}_{z}=0
and
{\bf E}_{z}|_{S}=0
and transverse electric (TE) modes with
{\bf E}_{z}=0
and
\partial{\bf B}_{z}/\partial n|_{S}=0
.
In the TE modes, the electric fields are transverse to the propagation
\hat{\bf z}
-direction with finite field components
\{E_{x},E_{y},H_{z}\}
. The magnetic field of the TM modes is transverse to the propagation direction and the field components are
\{H_{x},H_{y},E_{z}\}
.

We may separate the magnetic field of a mode
\lambda
with momentum
k>0
in the
\hat{\bf z}
-direction as

\displaystyle{\bf H}_{k}^{(\lambda)}(x,y,z)=\big[{\boldsymbol{\cal H}}_{k}^{(\mathrm{t},\lambda)}(x,y)+{\bf{\cal H}}_{kz}^{(\lambda)}(x,y)\hat{\bf z}\big]e^{ikz}/\sqrt{L},

(79a)

\displaystyle{\bf H}_{-k}^{(\lambda)}(x,y,z)=\big[-{\boldsymbol{\cal H}}_{k}^{(\mathrm{t},\lambda)}(x,y)+{\bf{\cal H}}_{kz}^{(\lambda)}(x,y)\hat{\bf z}\big]e^{-ikz}/\sqrt{L},

(79b)

where “
t
” means “transverse”, and
L\rightarrow\infty
is the length of the waveguide. When there are no charges in the waveguide
\nabla\cdot{\bf H}_{\pm k}^{(\lambda)}=0
. Analogous relations hold for the electric fields
\boldsymbol{\cal E}^{\lambda}_{\pm k}
. These modes satisfy the orthonormal relations
[
206
]
,

\displaystyle\int\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\cal H$\unboldmath}}_{k,x}^{\left(\lambda\right)\ast}{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal H$\unboldmath}}_{k,x}^{\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)}+{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal H$\unboldmath}}_{k,y}^{\left(\lambda\right)\ast}{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal H$\unboldmath}}_{k,y}^{\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)}\right)d\boldsymbol{\rho}=\frac{A_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)}}{(Z_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)})^{2}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},

(80a)

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{H}_{k,z}^{\left(\lambda\right)\ast}\mathcal{H}_{k,z}^{\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)}d\boldsymbol{\rho}=\frac{\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}A_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)}}{k^{2}(Z_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)})^{2}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},\hskip 11.38092pt(\mathrm{TE)}

(80b)

\displaystyle\int\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$\unboldmath}}_{k,x}^{\left(\lambda\right)\ast}{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$\unboldmath}}_{k,x}^{\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)}+{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$\unboldmath}}_{k,y}^{\left(\lambda\right)\ast}{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$\unboldmath}}_{k,y}^{\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)}\right)d\boldsymbol{\rho}=A_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},

(80c)

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{E}_{k,z}^{\left(\lambda\right)\ast}\mathcal{E}_{k,z}^{\left(\lambda^{\prime}\right)}d\boldsymbol{\rho}=\frac{\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}A_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)}}{k^{2}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}},\hskip 22.76228pt(\mathrm{TM)}

(80d)

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}=(x,y)
and we introduced the impedances
Z_{k}^{(\lambda)}=\mu_{0}\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)}/k
and
k/(\varepsilon_{0}\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)})
as well as
A_{k}^{(\lambda)}=\hbar\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)}/(2\varepsilon_{0})
and
\hbar/(2\varepsilon_{0}\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)})
for the TE and TM modes with frequency
\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)}
, respectively, while

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}=\mu_{0}\varepsilon_{0}(\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)})^{2}-k^{2}=(\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)})^{2}/c^{2}-k^{2}.

(81)

\gamma_{\lambda}
in
\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)}=c\sqrt{k^{2}+\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}}
only depends on the band index but not the momentum.

The electromagnetic fields can be expanded in the waveguide-photon operators
\hat{p}_{k}^{\lambda}
,

\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\lambda}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\boldsymbol{\cal H}_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)}(\boldsymbol{\rho})e^{ikz}\hat{p}_{k}^{\left(\lambda\right)}+\mathrm{H.c.}\right)\frac{dk}{\sqrt{2\pi}},

(82)

and similarly for the electric field with
\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}
and
amplitudes
\boldsymbol{\cal H}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\cal E}
. The orthonormal relations (
80d
) lead to a compact expansion of the field energy

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\int d{\bf r}{\bf E}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf E}({\bf r})+\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\int d{\bf r}{\bf H}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf H}({\bf r})=\sum_{k,\lambda}\hbar\omega_{k}^{(\lambda)}(\hat{p}_{k,\lambda}^{\dagger}\hat{p}_{k,\lambda}+\hat{p}_{-k,\lambda}^{\dagger}\hat{p}_{-k,\lambda}).

(83)

At ideal metallic walls [Eq. (
78
)] the magnetic field is parallel to the boundary.
Therefore, for most traveling magnetic fields, it must have at least one vortex in the field lines.
We illustrate the physics for a rectangular waveguide with dimensions
a>b
,
focusing on the lowest TE
10
mode, where the subscripts 1 and 0 are indices of the two transverse Cartesian modes. Then
\gamma_{{\rm TE}_{10}}=\pi/a
,
Z_{k}=\mu_{0}\omega_{k}/k
and
A_{k}=\hbar\omega_{k}/(2\epsilon_{0})
,
and

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\pm k,x}=\mp\sqrt{\frac{2}{ab}}\frac{\sqrt{A_{k}}}{\mu_{0}\omega_{k}}\sqrt{\Big(\frac{\omega_{k}}{c}\Big)^{2}-\Big(\frac{\pi}{a}\Big)^{2}}\sin\Big(\frac{\pi x}{a}\Big),

(84a)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\pm k,y}=0,

(84b)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\pm k,z}=-i\sqrt{\frac{2}{ab}}\frac{\sqrt{A_{k}}}{\mu_{0}\omega_{k}}\frac{\pi}{a}\cos\Big(\frac{\pi x}{a}\Big).

(84c)

Of special interest is the existence of 2 “chiral planes (lines in side view)” along the waveguide at which the magnetic fields are circularly polarized in the
(x,z)
plane. Its precise location depends on the waveguide geometry and the polarization on the 2 planes is opposite. The sign of
{\cal H}_{\pm k,x}
depends on the propagation direction which means that the polarization and momentum are locked.
Figure
21
(a) shows a snapshot of the magnetic-field vector distribution over one wavelength, in which the chiral planes are indicated by the green and red dashed lines, and we see that on these lines the magnetic field vector of constant modulus rotates clockwise and anticlockwise.

Figure 21:
Chirality of microwaves in a rectangular waveguide and a torus-shaped cavity. (a) A snapshot of the magnetic-field vector distribution of the TE
10
waveguide mode over one wavelength
\lambda
in the open
\hat{\bf z}
-direction. The green and red dashed lines in the side view indicate lines (planes) at which the magnetic field rotates with constant modulus,
i.e.
, is (oppositely) circularly polarized. (b) sketches the geometry of a torus cavity and the snapshot of the magnetic-field vector distribution of the lowest TE mode. In the enlarged cavity section indicating the polar angle
\phi
,
\rho_{+}
and
\rho_{-}
indicate the chiral lines (planes) of the magnetic field that are calculated by Eq. (
95
), while
\rho_{0}
is for the position of a linearly polarized magnetic field. The figures are taken from Refs.
[
60
,
63
]
.

Torus cavity
.—Bending a straight waveguide into a circle generates a closed, torus-shaped cavity
[
252
,
253
]
. It is easily seen that in the limit of a large aspect ratio the chiral lines (planes) should at which the polarization is locked to the “momentum” form closed circles at approximately equal distance from the boundaries. The electric field of the TM modes of the torus-like cavity with inner
and outer radii
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
and height
b
in the coordinate system of Fig.
21
(b)
with
{\bf E}_{t}={\bf E}-{E}_{z}\hat{\mathbf{z}}=0
at
z=0
and
z=b
read in cylindrical
coordinates
\{\rho,\phi\}

{\bf E}_{z}=\psi(\rho,\phi)\cos\left(\frac{p\pi z}{b}\right),~~~~~p=0,1,2,\cdots.

(85)

with the radial wave equation

(\nabla_{t}^{2}+\gamma_{p}^{2})\psi_{p}=0,

(86)

where
\gamma_{p}^{2}=\omega^{2}/c^{2}-(p\pi/b)^{2}
and the index
p
labels the standing waves in the
\hat{\bf z}
-direction. With the cylindrical coordinates,

\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}\left(\rho\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\rho}\right)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\gamma_{p}^{2}\psi=0.

(87)

With
\psi(\rho,\phi)=R_{m}(\rho)e^{im\phi}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{0}
, we arrive at a Bessel equation of order
m
:

\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}\left(\rho\frac{\partial R_{m}(\rho)}{\partial\rho}\right)+\left(\gamma_{p,m}^{2}-\frac{m^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)R_{m}(\rho)=0,

(88)

with a general solution

R_{m}(\rho)=J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}\rho)+CN_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}\rho),

(89)

where
J_{m}(x)
and
N_{m}(x)
are the Bessel functions of the first and second
kind, respectively. The boundary conditions with zero tangential
E_{z}
at the inner and outer walls

\displaystyle J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{2})+CN_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{2})

\displaystyle=0,

(90a)

\displaystyle J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})+CN_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})

\displaystyle=0,

(90b)

lead to
C=-J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})/N_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1}),

J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{2})N_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})=J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})N_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{2}),

(91)

and an electric field along
z

{\cal E}_{z}(\rho,\phi,z)=\left(J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}\rho)-\frac{J_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})}{N_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}R_{1})}N_{m}(\gamma_{p,m}\rho)\right)e^{im\phi}\cos\left(\frac{p\pi z}{b}\right),

(92)

which still has to be normalized. According to the Maxwell equations
(\nabla\times{\bf B})=(1/c^{2})\partial_{t}{\bf E}
and
\nabla\times{\bf E}=-\partial_{t}{\bf B}
, the magnetic field of the TM mode

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{x}(\rho,\phi,z)

\displaystyle=-i\frac{\omega}{\mu_{0}\gamma_{p,m}^{2}c^{2}}\partial_{y}{\cal E}_{z}=-i\frac{\omega}{\mu_{0}\gamma_{p,m}^{2}c^{2}}\left(\sin\phi\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}+\frac{1}{\rho}\cos\phi\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\right){\cal E}_{z},

(93a)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{y}(\rho,\phi,z)

\displaystyle=i\frac{\omega}{\mu_{0}\gamma_{p,m}^{2}c^{2}}\partial_{x}{\cal E}_{z}=i\frac{\omega}{\mu_{0}\gamma_{p,m}^{2}c^{2}}\left(\cos\phi\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\sin\phi\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\right){\cal E}_{z}.

(93b)

Figure
21
(b) shows the lowest standing mode (
p=0
).
In cylindrical coordinates
\boldsymbol{\cal H}(\rho,\phi)={\cal H}_{\rho}{\bf e}_{\rho}+{\cal H}_{\phi}{\bf e}_{\phi}
with components

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\rho}(\rho,\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}\gamma_{m}c}\frac{m}{\rho}{\cal E}_{z},

(94a)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\phi}(\rho,\phi)

\displaystyle=-i\frac{1}{\mu_{0}\gamma_{m}c}\frac{\partial{\cal E}_{z}}{\partial\rho}.

(94b)

We are now interested in the locking between the polarization and momentum, which is now not governed by the wave vector but by the orbital angular momentum that is governed by the integer
m
that indicates clockwise (CW,
m>0
) or counter-clockwise (CCW,
m<0
) motion. The rotation direction is “locked” to the momentum by
\pm|m|
at the “chiral lines”
\rho_{\pm}
in Fig.
21
(b) that solve

\frac{m}{\rho_{\pm}}{\cal E}_{z}(\rho_{\pm})+\left.\frac{\partial{\cal E}_{z}(\rho)}{\partial\rho}\right|_{\rho=\rho_{\pm}}=0,

(95)

since then magnetic field is circularly polarized
H_{\phi}=i\mathrm{sgn}(m)H_{\rho}
. For dimensions of tens of millimeters, the cavity is in GHz regime,
e.g.
,
with
R_{1}=15
mm and
R_{2}=30
mm, by solving
Eq. (
18
) to find
\gamma_{p,m}
, we find the
frequency

\displaystyle\omega_{m}=c\gamma_{p=0,m}=\{10.84,11.87,13.16,14.63,16.22,17.88,19.59,21.33,23.08\}~{\rm GHz}

(96)

when

m=\{2,3,...,10\}
.

The energy of the electromagnetic field is

\hat{H}_{p}=\int d{\bf r}\left[\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}{\bf E}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf E}({\bf r})+\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}{\bf H}({\bf r})\cdot{\bf H}({\bf r})\right].

(97)

We may quantize the fields for our TM mode in terms of the photon operator
\hat{\alpha}_{p,m}
,

\displaystyle{\bf H}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}\sum_{m}\left[\boldsymbol{\cal H}_{m}^{p}(\rho,z)e^{im\phi}\hat{\alpha}_{p,m}+\boldsymbol{\cal H}_{m}^{p*}(\rho,z)e^{-im\phi}\hat{\alpha}^{\dagger}_{p,m}\right],

(98a)

\displaystyle{\bf E}({\bf r})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}\sum_{m}\left[\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{m}^{p}(\rho,z)e^{im\phi}\hat{\alpha}_{p,m}+\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{m}^{p*}(\rho,z)e^{-im\phi}\hat{\alpha}^{\dagger}_{p,m}\right],

(98b)

such that Eq. (
97
) assumes the form of a collection of harmonic oscillators
\hat{H}_{p}=\sum_{p,m}\hbar\omega_{p,m}\hat{\alpha}^{\dagger}_{p,m}\hat{\alpha}_{p,m}
. The normalization conditions

\displaystyle\int d{\bf r}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}|\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{m}^{p}(\rho,z)|^{2}+\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}|\boldsymbol{\cal H}_{m}^{p}(\rho,z)|^{2}\right)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{p,m}}{2}

(99)

read in cylindrical coordinates

\displaystyle\pi\int_{R_{1}}^{R_{2}}\rho d\rho\int_{0}^{b}dz\left(\varepsilon_{0}|\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{m}^{p}(\rho,z)|^{2}+\mu_{0}|\boldsymbol{\cal H}_{m}^{p}(\rho,z)|^{2}\right)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{p,m}}{2}.

(100)

The field distribution of small torus cavities and therefore large curvature differs from straight open waveguides. The maxima of the magnetic field and the chiral lines shift to the outer edges. Increasing the circumference of the torus at constant cross-section shifts the maximum field into its center, but also reduces the local amplitude of a given cavity mode at equal power. The straight waveguide limit is reached when the discrete
m
-modes merge into a one-dimensional continuum.

3.3
Chiral electric fields

In the microwave regime, the magnetic field component of electromagnetic radiation dominates the interaction with matter. At optical frequencies, the magnetic field can be disregarded and the electric field response takes over. Evanescent electric fields play a central role in (nano)optics of structures with sub-wavelength dimensions. As discussed above evanescent electric fields can be expanded into plane waves of the form
{\bf E}e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\bf r}-\omega t}
in which at least one component of the wave vector
k
is imaginary and the field amplitude decays exponentially.

3.3.1
Surface plasmon polaritons

The surface plasmon polariton (SPP) is a hybrid of the electromagnetic field and collective motion of electrons at the surfaces of metals, insulators with a large dielectric constant, or mismatched material interfaces
[
254
,
255
,
256
,
257
]
. Its amplitude decays exponentially as a function of distance from the surface or interface, while the propagation direction is governed by the outer product of the surface normal and polarization,
i.e.
, exhibiting chirality, as explained in more detail below.

Bulk plasmon
.—We first recall the concepts of bulk plasmon and its interaction with electromagnetic waves. Maxwell equations read

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot{\bf D}

\displaystyle=\rho_{f},

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot{\bf B}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bf E}

\displaystyle=-{\partial{\bf B}}/{\partial t},

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bf H}

\displaystyle={\bf J}_{f}+{\partial{\bf D}}/{\partial t},

(101)

where
\rho_{f}
is the density of net free charges that contribute to a free charge current
{\bf J}_{f}
via the conservation law
\partial\rho_{f}/\partial t=-\nabla\cdot{\bf J}_{f}
, the electric displacement field
{\bf D}=\varepsilon_{0}{\bf E}+{\bf P}
, and
{\bf H}={\bf B}/\mu_{0}-{\bf M}
. On the other hand, the electric polarization
{\bf P}
is related to the bounded charge accumulation
\rho_{b}
according to

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot{\bf P}=-\rho_{b}.

(102)

The charge conservation
\nabla\cdot{\bf J}_{b}=-\partial\rho_{b}/\partial t
defines the associated bounded charge current
{\bf J}_{b}
that leads to the displacement current

\displaystyle{\bf J}_{b}={\partial{\bf P}}/{\partial t}.

(103)

Here we focus on the electrodynamics of isotropic materials in the linear response regime. In the frequency and momentum space

\displaystyle{\bf D}({\bf k},\omega)=\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega){\bf E}({\bf k},\omega),

(104a)

\displaystyle{\bf J}({\bf k},\omega)=\sigma({\bf k},\omega){\bf E}({\bf k},\omega),

(104b)

where
\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)
is the dielectric constant and
\sigma({\bf k},\omega)
is the dynamic conductivity. The Maxwell equations (
101
) with Eq. (
103
) lead to

\displaystyle\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)=1+\frac{i\sigma({\bf k},\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}.

(105)

The imaginary part of
\sigma({\bf k},\omega)
contributes to the real part of
\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)
,
i.e.
, the ability for a material to be polarized by the electric field; while the real part of
\sigma({\bf k},\omega)
corresponds to the imaginary part of
\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)
,
i.e.
, the ability of a material to absorb electromagnetic waves.

At the zeros of the dielectric constant
\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)={\bf D}=0
, the polarization
{\bf P}
exactly cancels the electric field
{\bf E}
, which implies that the motion of the electrons becomes collective. The root of
\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)=0
defines the dispersion of this collective mode or “plasmon”.
The Lindhard formula for the dielectric constant of free electrons in bulk metals reads
[
258
]

\displaystyle\varepsilon({\bf q},\omega)=1+\frac{8\pi e^{2}}{q^{2}}\sum_{{\bf k}}n_{\bf k}\frac{E_{{\bf k}+{\bf q}}-E_{\bf k}}{(E_{{\bf k}+{\bf q}}-E_{\bf k})^{2}-\omega^{2}},

(106)

where
n_{\bf k}
is the Fermi distribution and
E_{\bf k}
is the electronic band structure. In the long-wavelength limit with
{\bf q}\rightarrow 0
, the plasmon dispersion is the root of

\displaystyle\varepsilon({\bf q},\omega_{q})=1-\frac{\omega_{\rm P}^{2}}{\omega_{q}^{2}}-\frac{3\omega_{\rm P}^{2}}{5\omega_{q}^{4}}q^{2}v_{F}^{2}=0,

(107)

where
\omega_{\rm P}=\sqrt{{4\pi ne^{2}}/{m}}
is the plasmon frequency from the Drude model, and
v_{F}
is the Fermi velocity. The plasmon frequency in bulk metal is then obtained to be

\displaystyle\omega_{q}=\omega_{\rm P}\sqrt{1+\frac{3v_{F}^{2}}{5\omega_{\rm P}^{2}}q^{2}},

(108)

with a frequency gap
\omega_{\rm P}
.

Plasmon polariton
.—The bulk plasmon couples strongly with the optical electric field to form a hybrid plasmon polariton. The wave equation for the electric field in metals follows from the Maxwell equations (
101
) and Eq. (
104a
):

\displaystyle{\bf k}({\bf k}\cdot{\bf E})-k^{2}{\bf E}=-\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}{\bf E}.

(109)

The longitudinal mode with
{\bf k}\cdot{\bf E}=kE
, leads to
\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)=0
,
i.e.
, corresponding to the bare plasmon excitation. The plasmon polariton is therefore associated with the transverse mode with
{\bf k}\cdot{\bf E}=0
that satisfies

\displaystyle k^{2}=\varepsilon({\bf k},\omega)\omega^{2}/c^{2},

(110)

which leads to its dispersion relation.

In the long-wave length limit of a metal
\varepsilon_{m}(\omega)\rightarrow 1-\omega_{\rm P}^{2}/\omega^{2}
, Eq. (
110
) leads to the frequency of plasmon polariton

\omega_{\rm PP}=\sqrt{\omega_{\rm P}^{2}+k^{2}c^{2}}.

(111)

The light is then not allowed to propagate below
\omega_{P}
in the bulk because
{\bf k}
thereby becomes imaginary. For an optical field of
\omega>\omega_{P}
, only the transverse plasmon couples to the optical field which leads to a hybridization.

Surface plasmon polariton
.—Surface plasmon polariton emerges at the interface between a metal and a material with dielectric constant
\varepsilon_{d}
(or surface to the vacuum with
\varepsilon_{d}=1
). For
a flat interface between two semi-infinite spaces of a conductor (
y\leq 0
) and a dielectric (
y>0
), as in Fig.
22
(a), the Maxwell’s equations in frequency space read

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bf E}

\displaystyle=i\omega\mu_{0}{\bf H},

(112a)

\displaystyle\nabla\times{\bf H}

\displaystyle=-i\omega\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}(y){\bf E},

(112b)

where
\varepsilon_{r}(y\leq 0)=\varepsilon_{m}
at the metal and
\varepsilon_{r}(y>0)=\varepsilon_{d}
at the dielectric side. The solutions are planes waves that we assume to propagate along the
\hat{\bf x}
-direction,
i.e.
,
\boldsymbol{\cal H}(y)e^{ik_{x}x}
and
\boldsymbol{\cal E}(y)e^{ik_{x}x}
. The TE mode with
{\bf E}=E_{z}\hat{\bf z}
and
{\bf H}=H_{x}\hat{\bf x}+H_{y}\hat{\bf y}
does not solve Eqs. (
112b
) and (
112a
)
[
257
]
, so the SSP must be a TM mode with
{\bf E}=E_{x}\hat{\bf x}+E_{y}\hat{\bf y}
and
{\bf H}=H_{z}\hat{\bf z}
with wave equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}H_{z}}{\partial y^{2}}+(\varepsilon_{r}k_{0}^{2}-k_{x}^{2})H_{z}=0,

(113)

where
k_{0}^{2}=\omega^{2}/c^{2}
. Since the in-plane magnetic field is continuous across the interface,

\displaystyle H_{z}(x,y)=Ae^{ik_{x}x}e^{-\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}-\varepsilon_{r}k_{0}^{2}}y}.

(114)

The electric field components

\displaystyle E_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\omega\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}}\frac{\partial H_{z}}{\partial y}=-\frac{i}{\omega\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}-\varepsilon_{r}k_{0}^{2}}H_{z},

(115a)

\displaystyle E_{y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\omega\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}}\frac{\partial H_{z}}{\partial x}=\frac{k_{x}}{\omega\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}}H_{z},

(115b)

are related as
E_{x}=-iE_{y}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}-\varepsilon_{r}k_{0}^{2}}/k_{x}
. When
k_{x}\gg k_{0}
,
E_{y}=i{\rm sgn}(k_{x})E_{x}
,
i.e.
, a polarization-momentum locking of the electric field as illustrated in Fig.
22
(a).

Figure 22:
Chirality and dispersion of surface plasmon polaritons. (a) illustrates the polarization-momentum locking of the electric field of surface plasmon polaritons: the electric field is right (left) circularly polarized for the right (left) moving excitations. (b) plots the dispersion of surface plasmon polaritons.
k_{0}=\omega_{\rm SPP}/c
is indicated.

We can derive the dispersion relation
\omega_{\rm SPP}(k_{x})
from the boundary conditions,
i.e.
, the continuity of the normal components of
{\bf D}
(and
{\bf B}
) and that of the tangential components of
{\bf E}
(and
{\bf H}
). From
E_{x}^{(m)}=E_{x}^{(d)}
,

\displaystyle\omega_{\rm SPP}=\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon_{d}+\varepsilon_{m}(\omega_{\rm SPP})}{\varepsilon_{d}\varepsilon_{m}(\omega_{\rm SPP})}}c|k_{x}|.

(116)

Equation (
114
) is a surface mode if
\varepsilon_{r}k_{0}^{2}<k_{x}^{2}
. Therefore
\varepsilon_{m}(\omega_{\rm SPP})<0
and
\omega_{\rm SPP}<\omega_{\rm P}
. Moreover,
\omega_{\rm SPP}
must be real and positive, which requires
\varepsilon(\omega_{\rm SPP})+\varepsilon_{d}<0
and
\omega_{\rm SPP}<\omega_{\rm P}/\sqrt{1+\varepsilon_{d}}
. These features are summarized in Fig.
22
(b). The upper branch, if we wonder, is just the dispersion of plasmon polariton Eq. (
111
), which is bulk in nature.

SPPs allow sub-wavelength confinement of optical fields. Plasmonic structures act as antennae of electromagnetic radiation that strongly enhance the amplitude of incident signals. As reviewed in Sec.
5.2
, SPPs can generate electronic spin currents in normal metals
[
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
91
]
.

3.3.2
Stray fields by dynamic electric dipoles

Analogous to the magnetic dipoles discussed in the previous Sec.
3.2.2
, electric dipoles emit stray electric fields that become chiral when evanescent. They are important in the near-field regime of nano-optics
[
17
]
. Here we discuss the waves emitted by electric dipoles that oscillate or rotate in a plane normal to an interface, as formulated by Fortuño
et. al.

[
19
]
.

According to non-relativistic radiation theory
[
206
]
, Eq. (
29
), an electric free or bounded current
J
generates a vector potential
{\bf A}({\bf r},t)=({\mu_{0}}/4\pi)\int d{\bf r}^{\prime}{\bf J}({\bf r}^{\prime},t){e^{ik|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}}/{|{\bf r}-{\bf r}^{\prime}|}
. A uniform line of dipoles
{\bf p}=p_{x}\hat{\bf x}+p_{z}\hat{\bf z}
along the
\hat{\bf y}
-direction that oscillates with frequency
\omega
generates a displacement current

{\bf J}(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})=-i\omega\delta(x^{\prime},z^{\prime}){\bf p}.

(117)

Its vector potential

\displaystyle{\bf A}({\bf r},t)=\frac{\mu_{0}}{4\pi}\int dy^{\prime}\frac{-i\omega{\bf p}}{\sqrt{x^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}+z^{2}}}e^{ik\sqrt{x^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}+z^{2}}}

(118)

does not depend on
y
as can be made explicit by using the Weyl identity (
30
),

{\bf A}(x,z)=\frac{\mu_{0}\omega}{4\pi}{\bf p}\int dk_{x}\frac{e^{ik_{x}x+ik_{z}|z|}}{k_{z}}.

(119)

The vector potential is parallel to
{\bf p}
,
and the magnetic field
{\bf H}({\bf r})=({1}/{\mu_{0}})\nabla\times{\bf A}({\bf r})=(0,H_{y},0)

becomes for
z<0

H_{y}(x,z)=\int{dk_{x}}e^{ik_{x}x}H_{y}(k_{x},z),

(120)

where
k_{z}=\sqrt{(\omega/c)^{2}-k_{x}^{2}}
and

H_{y}(k_{x},z)=-\frac{i\omega}{4\pi}\Big(p_{x}+\frac{k_{x}}{k_{z}}p_{z}\Big)e^{-ik_{z}z}.

(121)

The magnetic field generated by an in-plane dipole is always linearly polarized. When
k_{x}\gg\omega/c\equiv k_{0}
, it becomes evanescent with

H_{y}(k_{x},z)=-\frac{i\omega}{4\pi}\Big(p_{x}-i{\rm sgn}({k_{x}})p_{z}\Big)e^{-|k_{x}||z|}.

(122)

When the dipole
rotates
with
p_{z}=ip_{x}
, this Fourier component

H_{y}(k_{x},z)=-\frac{i\omega}{4\pi}p_{x}\Big(1+{\rm sgn}({k_{x}})\Big)e^{-|k_{x}||z|}

(123)

is unidirectional,
viz
., it vanishes when
k_{x}<0
and
H_{y}(k_{x},z)e^{ik_{x}x}
is a linear polarized traveling wave with a chirality governed by the rotation direction of the dipole.

According to Eq. (
112b
), the associated electric field in the evanescent limit (
z<0
)

\displaystyle E_{x}(k_{x},z)=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}}\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(p_{x}-i{\rm sgn}(k_{x})p_{z}\right)|k_{x}|e^{-|k_{x}||z|},

(124a)

\displaystyle E_{z}(k_{x},z)=\frac{i}{\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{r}}\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(p_{x}-i{\rm sgn}(k_{x})p_{z}\right)k_{x}e^{-|k_{x}||z|},

(124b)

i.e.
,
E_{z}(k_{x},z)=i{\rm sgn}(k_{x})E_{x}(k_{x},z)
. A linearly polarized electric dipole generates a circularly polarized electric field wave that propagates in both directions, with polarization (spin) locked to the momentum. On the other hand, a circularly polarized electric dipole emits a (circularly polarized) electric field in only one direction that is locked to the rotation direction of the dipole, i.e., being unidirectional.

From the above discussion and Sec.
3.3.1
we understand that the dynamics of an electric dipole close to a metal surface may chirally excite surface plasmon polaritons, as reported by Rodríguez-Fortuño
et al.

[
19
]
. As illustrated in Fig.
23
(a), a line of in-plane dipoles can be created by illuminating a narrow slit (130 nm) in a thin gold film by circularly or linearly polarized light. At grazing incidence, the circular polarization of the light’s electric field is approximately perpendicular to the metal interface. The excited SPPs are recorded by their leakage radiation on either side of the slit. As shown in Fig.
23
(b), the plasmons escape symmetrically to both sides for a linearly polarized dipole excitation, but the excitation becomes unidirectional with increasing polarization.

Figure 23:
Directional excitation of surface plasmon polaritons by local electric-dipole “wire”. (a) is the schematics of the experiment, where a plane wave is an incident at a nearly grazing angle (
70^{\degree}
) onto a 130 nm slit in a thin gold film. The excitation of the surface plasmon polariton is recorded by the leakage radiation from either side of the slit. The results in Fourier space in (b) show the dependence of the excitation direction on the light polarization. The figures are taken from Ref.
[
19
]
.

3.4
Chiral phonons

The excitations of elastic media such as ultrasound obey wave equations that are similar, but also characteristically different from those of electromagnetic radiation or the magnetic order. It should come as no surprise by now that also sound and lattice vibrations display chiral properties when evanescent. Continuum mechanics is appropriate to describe pressure and shear waves at long wavelengths, while at short wavelengths the atomic dynamics should be taken into account. The quanta of mechanical excitations are phonons. Their eigenmodes are found by solving the lattice dynamical problem,
i.e.
, the classical equation of motion of the atomic displacement field. We consider a lattice periodic system with
r
atoms per unit cell located at
{\bf R}_{l}+{\bf d}_{s}
, where
{\bf R}_{l}
is the origin of the
l
-th unit cell and
{\bf d}_{s}
is the position of the
s
-th atoms in this unit cell. Their displacement into the
\alpha
-direction
{u}_{l}^{\alpha}(s,t)
so obeys the (linearized) equation of motion
[
259
]

\displaystyle M_{a}u_{l}^{\alpha}(s)=-\sum_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}\beta}\Phi_{\alpha\beta}\left(\begin{matrix}l&l^{\prime}\\
s&s^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)u_{l^{\prime}}^{\beta}(s^{\prime}),

(125)

where
M_{a}
is the atomic mass and the
3r\times 3r
matrix
\Phi_{\alpha\beta}\left(\begin{matrix}l&l^{\prime}\\
s&s^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)
contains the force constants between different atoms. With an ansatz of a plane wave solution with wave vector
k
, the
3r
degrees of freedom in the unit cell lead to the same number of eigenvalues or Eq. (
125
) that we label as
\omega_{j=\{1,2,\cdots,3r\}}({\bf k})
. These form
3r
branches of phonons in the reciprocal space, including 3 acoustic and
(3r-3)
optical phonon bands, and the
3r
eigenvectors
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{(j)}_{s}({\bf k})
indicate the phonon polarization. In second quantization, the bosonic phonon operators
\hat{b}_{\bf k}
and
\hat{b}_{\bf k}^{\dagger}
govern the displacement field for atom
s
in unit cell
l

\displaystyle\hat{\bf u}_{l}(s)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{NM_{a}}}\sum_{{\bf k}}\sum_{j=1}^{3r}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2\omega_{j}({\bf k})}}\left(\hat{b}_{{\bf k}j}+\hat{b}_{-{\bf k}j}^{\dagger}\right)\boldsymbol{\xi}_{s}^{(j)}({\bf k})e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\bf R}_{l}},

(126)

where
N
is the number of unit cells. The phonon Hamiltonian is a distributed set of quantum harmonic oscillators
\hat{H}=\sum_{{\bf k}}\sum_{j}\hbar\omega_{j}({\bf k})(\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{{\bf k}j}\hat{b}_{{\bf k}j}+1/2)
.

Phonons have been called chiral
[
260
]
when i) their polarization is circular,
i.e.
, the displacement field rotates around the equilibrium position, and ii) the direction of the polarization rotation (spin) is locked to the linear momentum under the time-reversal symmetry. We focus here on two types of chiral phonons,
i.e.
, bulk phonons (Sec.
3.4.1
) and surface acoustic waves (Sec.
3.4.2
).

3.4.1
Chiral bulk phonons

A chirality of phonon modes in a bulk crystal affects the electron scattering
[
261
]
and the interaction with light
[
262
]
. In the reduced zone scheme the phonon crystal momenta lie in the first Brillouin zone and the phonon eigenmodes of the
j
-th band are the Bloch waves

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{(j)}_{l}({\bf k})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{NM_{a}}}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2\omega_{j}({\bf k})}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(j)}({\bf k})e^{i{\bf k}\cdot{\bf R}_{l}}.

(127)

These basis functions transform according to the point group symmetry of the lattice spanned by a number of symmetry operations
\hat{O}_{g}
such that

\hat{O}_{g}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{(i)}_{l}({\bf k})=R_{ji}(g){\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{l}^{(j)}(g^{-1}{\bf k})
in terms of the irreducible representation matrix
R_{ji}(g)
. At high-symmetry points of the Brillouin zone, eigenmodes tend to have degenerate eigenvalues and higher-dimensional irreducible representations of the point group. The phonon polarization is then not well-defined as an arbitrary superposition of the polarization of the degenerate states. A definite polarization of the phonons at symmetry points can be generated by breaking a symmetry. Zhang
et al
. showed that breaking the inversion symmetry generates a “pseudoangular” momentum of phonons at special points in the Brillouin zone of a two-dimensional honeycomb lattice such as monolayer transition-metal dichalcogenide
[
260
]
. The “pseudo-angular” momentum should not be confused with the “phonon spin”,
i.e.
, the rotational motion of a single atom that we discuss below. The “pseudo-angular” momentum is defined by the transformation law of the displacement field under the rotation operation, but not that defined via Noether’s theorem below. Throughout this review, we stick to the latter description as addressed in Sec.
3.4.3
for the phonon spins and orbital angular momentum.

We illustrate these abstract notions for a monolayer of a hexagonal lattice
[
260
]
. With
r=2
atoms, labeled by
A
and
B
in the unit cell, we have
3r=6
phonon modes of every momentum in total. Limiting attention to the in-plane motion, 2 acoustic and 2 optical phonon branches remain. At the Brillouin-zone corners points
{\bf K}
and
{\bf K}^{\prime}
, the phonon amplitudes transform according to the
C_{3h}
symmetry group that contains six elements including, in particular, the threefold rotation
C_{3}
, as shown in Table
3
together with their basis functions. All six representations
\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4},A_{5},A_{6}\}
are one-dimensional and non-degenerate. The basis of the representations
A_{3}
and
A_{4}
are right-handed and left-handed, respectively, thus corresponding to circular polarization and pseudo-angular momentum. They are not degenerate because of the lack of inversion symmetry. The threefold rotation of the basis at
{\bf K}
,

\hat{O}_{C_{3}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{l}^{(j)}({\bf K})={\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{l}^{(j)}(C_{3}^{-1}{\bf K})=e^{-2\pi l_{s}^{(j)}i/3}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{l}^{(j)}({\bf K})
therefore implies a pseudoangular momentum
l_{s}^{(j)}
of the band with index
j
. For the representations
A_{3}
and
A_{4}
,
l_{s}^{(A_{3})}=1
and
l_{s}^{(A_{4})}=-1
, while
l_{s}^{(j)}=0
for all others. These pseudoangular momenta are governed by the crystal symmetry and generate selection rules in the matrix elements of electron-phonon scattering
[
261
]
and photon-phonon
[
262
]
scattering. Figure
24
(a) shows the dispersion of the four phonon bands of the in-plane motion along the
K^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-
K
lines
[
260
]
. The motion of the
A
and
B
atoms in each band at
K
and
K^{\prime}
valleys are illustrated in the insets. Here bands 2 and 3 at the
{\bf K}
-valley has pseudoangular momentum
+1
and
-1
, respectively, with opposite sign at the
{\bf K}^{\prime}
-valley, as shown in Fig.
24
(b). There are more complicated cases in three dimensions and other symmetry groups
[
264
]
.

Table 3:
Character table for
C_{3h}
group
[
263
]
.

.

Dispersion relation [(a)] of phonon modes with in-plane dynamics and their pseudoangular momentum [(b)] at the
K
and
K^{\prime}
valleys of a two-dimensional honeycomb lattice of monolayer transition-metal dichalcogenides. The insets in (a) show the motions of the
A
and

atoms in every band at
K
and
K^{\prime}
valleys. These figures are taken from Ref.
[
